<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{ex:ud}
    Let $W(N) = e^{\sqrt{N}}$ for $N\in \mathbb{N}$. Theorem \ref{thm:W_ud} below implies the following.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item For the sequence $x_n = e^{2\pi i \log(n)}$ for $n\in \mathbb{N}$
        \begin{equation*}
            \lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\log}_{n\leqslant N}x_n=0, \quad  
        \lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}x_n \text{ does not exist, }\quad  
        \lim_{N\to\infty}  \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^W_{n\leqslant N}x_n \text{ does not exist.}
        \end{equation*}
        \item For the sequence $y_n = e^{2\pi i n\log(n)}$ for $n\in \mathbb{N}$
        \begin{equation*}
            \lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\log}_{n\leqslant N}y_n=0,\quad \quad  
        \lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}y_n=0 \quad \quad 
        \lim_{N\to\infty}  \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^W_{n\leqslant N}y_n \text{ does not exist.}
        \end{equation*}
        \item For the sequence $z_n = e^{2\pi  i n\sqrt{2}}$ for $n\in \mathbb{N}$,
        \begin{equation*}
            \lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\log}_{n\leqslant N}z_n=0,\quad \quad  
        \lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}z_n=0 \quad \quad 
        \lim_{N\to\infty}  \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^W_{n\leqslant N}z_n=0 .
        \end{equation*}
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:degree}
    Suppose that $f$ belongs to a Hardy field. 
                                                    
    If there exists a $d\in \mathbb{N}$ such that $|f(x)|\preceq x^{d}$, then we define $\deg(f)$ to be the minimal such value of $d$ and we say that $f$ is \emph{subpolynomial}.
    Additionally, we put $\deg^*(f) = \min\{\deg(f-q):q(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]\}$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:Hardy_szemeredi}
    Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field. Suppose that $f\in \mathcal{H}$ satisfies $x^{d-1}\prec f(x)\prec x^{d}$ for some $d\in \mathbb{N}$. Then for any $\ell\in \mathbb{N}$ and any invertible measure preserving system $(X,\mathscr{B},\mu,T)$ and any $A\in \mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A)>0$, the set 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Hardy_sz_returns_1}
   \{n:\mu(A\cap T^{-[ f(n)]}A\cap T^{-2[ f(n)]}A\cap \cdots\cap T^{-\ell[ f(n)]}A )>0\}
\end{equation}
is nonempty.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:thick_szemeredi}
    Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field. Suppose that $f\in \mathcal{H}$ satisfies $x^{d-1}\prec f(x)\prec x^{d}$ for some $d\in \mathbb{N}$. Then for any $\ell\in \mathbb{N}$ and any invertible measure preserving system $(X,\mathscr{B},\mu,T)$ and any $A\in \mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A)>0$, the set 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Hardy_sz_returns}
   \{n:\mu(A\cap T^{-[ f(n)]}A\cap T^{-2[ f(n)]}A\cap \cdots\cap T^{-\ell[ f(n)]}A )>0\}
\end{equation}
is thick, meaning that it contains arbitrarily long intervals of natural numbers.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
Let $R\subseteq \mathbb{R}[x]$ be a finite set of real polynomials. We say that $R$ is \emph{jointly intersective} if there exists a finite set $P\subseteq \mathbb{Z}[x]$ with $R\subseteq \text{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}(P)$ such that for each $r\in \mathbb{N}$ there exists $n\in \mathbb{N}$ such that $p(n)$ is divisible by $r$ for all $p(x)\in P$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
Equivalently, $R$ is jointly intersective if and only if $R$ is contained in the principle ideal of $\mathbb{R}[x]$ generated by $p_0(x)$, where $p_0(x)\in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ is a polynomial such that for each $r\in \mathbb{N}$ there is $n\in \mathbb{N}$ with $p_0(n)$ divisible by $r$ (cf. \cite[Proposition 6.1]{BLL08}). Moreover, if each polynomial contained in $R$ has constant term equal to $0$, then it is trivial to see that $R$ is jointly intersective because $R\subseteq (x)$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:polynomial_sz}
    Let $P= \{p_1,\dots, p_{\ell}\}\subseteq \mathbb{Z}[x]$. $P$ is jointly intersective if and only if for any invertible measure preserving system $(X,\mathscr{B},\mu,T)$ and any $A\in \mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A)>0$, 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:polynomial_sz}
R_A(p_1\dots, p_{\ell})=\{n\in \mathbb{N}: \mu(A\cap T^{-p_1(n)}A\cap\cdots \cap T^{-p_{\ell}(n)}A)>0\}
\end{equation}
is {syndetic}, meaning that it has bounded gaps.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:W_syndetic}
    Let $W$ be a function with $1\prec W(x)\preceq x$. We say that $S\subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is $W$\emph{-syndetic} if 
    \begin{equation}
        \liminf_{W(N)-W(M)\to\infty}\frac{1}{W(N)-W(M)}\sum_{n=M}^N\Delta W(n)1_{S}(n)>0.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:general_thick_szemeredi}
    Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field and let $f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\in \mathcal{H}$. Assume that for each $i\in \{1,\dots, \ell\}$ there exists $d\in \mathbb{N}$ with $1\prec f_i(x)\preceq x^{d}$. Let $W\in \mathcal{H}$ with $\log x\prec  W(x)\prec x$ such that 
    \begin{equation}
    \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f^{(d)}(x)-q(x)|^{1/d}}{W'(x)}>0
    \end{equation}
    for each $q(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ and each unbounded $f\in \text{Span}\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$, where $d = \deg^*(f)$. Define 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:poly_def}
         \operatorname{Poly}\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\} := \{p(x)\in \mathbb{R}[x]:\lim_{x\to\infty}|f(x)-p(x)|= 0\text{ for some } f\in  \text{Span}\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}\}.
\end{equation}
Suppose that $\operatorname{Poly}\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$ is jointly intersective. Then for any invertible probability measure preserving system $(X,\mathscr{B},\mu,T)$ and $A\in \mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A)>0$, the set 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general_Hardy_sz_returns}
    R_A(f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}):=\{n:\mu(A\cap T^{-[ f_1(n)]}A\cap T^{-[ f_2(n)]}A\cap \cdots\cap T^{-[ f_{\ell}(n)]}A )>0\}.
\end{equation}
is $W$-syndetic. In particular, $R_A(f_1,\dots, f_{\ell})$ is nonempty (a fact shown in \cite{BMR24}).
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def:W_compatible}
 Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field and let $W,f\in \mathcal{H}$. Suppose that $1\prec \log W(x)\prec x$ and that $f$ is subpolynomial. Let $d=\deg^*(f)$. We say that $W$ is \emph{compatible} with $f$ if either $d=0$ or $d>0$ and
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:W_compatible}
     \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f^{(d)}(x)-p(x)|^{1/d}}{(\log W)'(x)}=\infty.\tag{$\star$}
 \end{equation}
for all $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$. Let $f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\in \mathcal{H}$. We say that $\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$ satisfies property (\ref{eq:W_compatible}) if $W$ is compatible with each $f\in \text{Span}\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
Let $f(x) = x^c$ for $c\in (0,\infty)\backslash \mathbb{N}$. Then $W$ is compatible with $f$ so long as $\log W(x)$ grows slower than $x^{c/\lfloor c \rfloor}$. For instance, $W(x) = e^{x^{(3/4-\varepsilon)}}$ is compatible with $f(x)=x^{3/2}$ for each $\varepsilon>0$.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{remark:WP}
In \cite[Section 6]{BMR24} it is said that $W,f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}$ satisfy property (WP) if each $f_i$ is subpolynomial, $1\prec W(x)\preceq x$, and 
\begin{align*}
    &\text{ For each }f\in \text{Span}\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\} \text{ and } p(x)\in \mathbb{R}[x],\text{ either } |f(x)-p(x)|\preceq 1\\ &\text{ or } \log W(x)\prec |f(x)-p(x)|\tag{WP}.
\end{align*}
We observe that (WP) holds for any $f$ with $\deg^*(f)\geqslant 2$. If $1\prec W(x)\preceq x$ (in fact, if $1\prec \log W(x)\preceq \log x$) then property (\ref{eq:W_compatible}) also holds for any $f$ with $\deg^*(f)\geqslant 2$, which can be verified using L'H\^opital's rule. Indeed, for $\deg^*(f)= 2$ and $1\prec \log W(x)\preceq \log x$, and for any $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$, 
\begin{align*}
    &\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f''(x)-p(x)|^{1/2}}{(\log W)'(x)} = \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f''(x)-p(x)|^{1/2}}{x^{-1}}\cdot \frac{x^{-1}}{(\log W)'(x)}\\ 
    =& \left(\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f''(x)-p(x)|}{x^{-2}}\cdot \left(\frac{x^{-1}}{(\log W)'(x)}\right)^2\right)^{1/2}.
\end{align*}
We know that 
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f''(x)-p(x)|}{x^{-2}}=\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f(x)-P(x)|}{\ln(x)} =\infty,
\end{equation*}
where $P''(x)=p(x)$, since $\deg^*(f)=2$, and $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^{-1}}{(\log W)'(x)}>0$ by the assumption that $\log W(x)\preceq \log x$. This proves the case when $\deg^*(f)=2$ and the case when $\deg^*(f)>2$ follows since $|f^{(3)}(x)-p(x)|^{1/3}\succ |(f')^{(2)}-p(x)|^{1/2}$ whenever $|f^{(3)}(x)-p(x)|$ tends to $0$. This shows that property (\ref{eq:W_compatible}) holds when $\deg^*(f)\geqslant 2$ and $1\prec \log W(x)\preceq \log x$. 
Additionally, note that property (\ref{eq:W_compatible}) and (WP) are the same condition when $\deg^*(f)\leqslant 1$. Thus, it follows that in the case $1\prec W(x)\preceq x$, property (WP) as defined in \cite{BMR24} is equivalent to property (\ref{eq:W_compatible}) defined above.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- maintheorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:main}
     Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field. Let $f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\in \mathcal{H}$ be  subpolynomial and let $W\in \mathcal{H}$ satisfy $1\prec \log W(x)\prec x$. Suppose that $\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$ satisfies property (\ref{eq:W_compatible}). Let $(X,\mathscr{B},\mu,T)$ be an invertible measure preserving system.
     \begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
         \item For each $h_1,\dots, h_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(X)$,
         \begin{equation}\label{eq:Thm_A_1}
             \lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^W(T^{[ f_1(n) ]}h_1\cdots T^{[ f_{\ell}(n) ]}h_{\ell})
         \end{equation}
         exists in $L^2(X)$.
        \item \label{condition_1}Suppose that $\operatorname{Poly}(f_1,\dots, f_{\ell})\cap \mathbb{Z}[x] = \{0\}$. 
        Then for each $h_1,\dots, h_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(X)$,
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:Thm_A_2}
             \lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^W(T^{[ f_1(n) ]}h_1\cdots T^{[ f_{\ell}(n) ]}h_{\ell}) = \prod_{i=1}^{\ell}h_i^*
         \end{equation}
         where $h_i^*$ is the projection in $L^2(X)$ of $h_i$ onto the subspace of $T$ invariant functions.
         \item\label{condition_2} Suppose that $\operatorname{Poly}\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$ is jointly intersective.
         Then for any $A\in \mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A)>0$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Thm_A_3}
             \lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^W\mu(A\cap T^{-[ f_1(n) ]}A\cap \cdots \cap T^{-[ f_{\ell}(n) ]}A) >0.
         \end{equation}
     \end{enumerate}
<!-- end maintheorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:BMR_conj}
    Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field. Let $f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\in \mathcal{H}$ be  subpolynomial and let $W\in \mathcal{H}$ satisfy $1\prec W(x)\preceq x$. Suppose that $\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$ satisfies (WP) (see Remark \ref{remark:WP}). Then each part of Theorem \ref{thm:main} holds.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field and let $f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\in \mathcal{H}$ be subpolynomial. Suppose that if $f$ is a nontrivial linear combination of $f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}$ then $f$ satisfies $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f(x)-q(x)|}{\log x}=\infty$ for all $q(x)\in \mathbb{Z}[x]$. Then for any ergodic measure preserving system and any $h_1,\dots, h_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(X)$, the limit
    \[
    \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^N T^{[ f_1(n) ]}h_1\cdots T^{[ f_{\ell}(n) ]}h_{\ell}
    \] 
    converges to $\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\int h_i~d\mu$ in $L^2$.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- maintheorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:uniform_main}
     Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field, let $W\in \mathcal{H}$ satisfy $
     \log x\prec  W(x)\prec x$, and let $f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\in \mathcal{H}$ be subpolynomial. Suppose that 
     \begin{equation}\label{eq:thm_B_condition}
         \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f^{(d)}(x)-p(x)|^{1/d}}{W'(x)}>0
     \end{equation}
     for each $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ and each unbounded $f\in \text{Span}\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$, where $d = \deg^*(f)$.  
     Let $(X,\mathscr{B},\mu,T)$ be an invertible measure preserving system.
     \begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
         \item For each $h_1,\dots, h_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(X)$, the limit
         \begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform_A_1}
         \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\text{unif}}^{ W}(T^{[ f_1(n) ]}h_1\cdots T^{[ f_{\ell}(n) ]}h_{\ell})
         \end{equation}
         exists in $L^2(X)$.
        \item Suppose that $\operatorname{Poly}(f_1,\dots, f_{\ell})\cap \mathbb{Z}[x] = \{0\}$. Then for any $h_1,\dots, h_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(X)$,
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform_A_2}
            \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\text{unif}}^{ W}(T^{[ f_1(n) ]}h_1\cdots T^{[ f_{\ell}(n) ]}h_{\ell}) = \prod_{i=1}^{\ell}h_i^*
         \end{equation}
         where $h_i^*$ is the projection in $L^2(X)$ of $h_i$ onto the subspace of $T$ invariant functions. It follows that for $A\in \mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A)>0$ and for any $\varepsilon>0$, the set 
         $$
 \{n\in \mathbb{N}:\mu(A\cap T^{-[ f_1(n) ]}A\cap\cdots \cap T^{-[ f_{\ell}(n) ]}A)>\mu(A)^{k+1}-\varepsilon\}
         $$
         is $W$-syndetic.
         \item Suppose that $\operatorname{Poly}\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$ is jointly intersective. Then for any $A\in \mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A)>0$,
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform_A_3}
             \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\text{unif}}^{ W}\mu(A\cap T^{-[ f_1(n) ]}A\cap \cdots \cap T^{-[ f_{\ell}(n) ]}A) >0.
         \end{equation}
     \end{enumerate}
<!-- end maintheorem-2 -->
<!-- example-3: body_md -->
Let $W(N)={\sqrt{N}}$ and let $f_1(x) = x^{0.51}$ (or any power of $x$ larger than $x^{0.5}$) and $f_2(x)= x\cdot \log(x)^2$. It is straightforward to verify that (\ref{eq:thm_B_condition}) holds and that $\operatorname{Poly}\{f_1,f_2\}=\{0\}$. Then by Theorem \ref{thm:uniform_main}(iii)
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:intro_example}
    \lim_{\sqrt{N}-\sqrt{M}\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}-\sqrt{M}}\sum_{n=M}^N\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}\mu(A\cap T^{-[ n^{0.51} ]}A\cap  T^{-[ n\cdot \log(n)^2 ]}A) >0
    \end{equation}
    for any invertible $(X,\mathscr{B},\mu,T)$ and any $A\in \mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A)>0$. Let $E\subseteq \mathbb{N}$ be a set with $\overline{d}(E):=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}1_E(n) > 0$. Using (\ref{eq:intro_example}), Theorem \ref{thm:mikey_thm}, and Furstenberg's correspondence principle (Theorem \ref{thm:Furstenberg_correspondence} below), there exists a value $a\in E$ such that for all large enough $N\in \mathbb{N}$, there exists $n\in [N-N^{0.51},N]$ with 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:example}
   \{a,a+[ n\cdot \log(n)^2 ],a+[ n^{0.51} ]\}\subseteq \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}.
   \end{equation}
  Moreover, let $A$ be the set of all $a\in E$ for which (\ref{eq:example}) holds. Then $\overline{d}(A)>0$.
<!-- end example-3 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:mikey_thm}
Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a maximal Hardy field. Let $W\in \mathcal{H}$ and suppose that $\log(x)\prec  \log W(x)\prec x$. Let $Y$ be a Banach space, let $(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\subseteq Y$ be a bounded sequence, and let $L\in Y$. Then the following are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}[label = (\arabic*)]
\item $\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\text{unif}}^{\log W}x_n = L$.
\item $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^Ux_n = L$ for each $U\in\mathcal{H}$ with $1\prec \log U(x)\prec \log W(x)$.
\item $\lim_{N\to\infty} \frac{1}{s(N)}\sum_{n=N-s(N)}^Nx_n= L$ for each nondecreasing function $s:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}$ satisfying 
$
\lim_{N\to\infty}s(N) \cdot (\log W)'(N)=\infty
$
and $s(N)\leqslant N-1$ for all sufficiently large $N\in \mathbb{N}$.
\item $\lim_{k\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}|Y_{N,k}-L|=0$, where $Y_{N,0} = x_{N}$ and $Y_{N,k+1}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{W}_{n\leqslant N}Y_{n,k}$ for $N\in \mathbb{N}$ and $k\geqslant 0$.
\end{enumerate}
Moreover, if $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{W}_{n\leqslant N}x_n=L$ then each of the above statements hold.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{remark:1precW}
    The assumption that $\log x\prec \log W(x)$ is necessary for statement (3) to not be vacuous, but the implications $(1)\implies (4)\implies (2)$ are each proven in \cite[Corollary 2.2, Corollary 3.3, Theorem 4.1, Theorem 5.5]{reilly26} under the more general assumption that $1\prec \log W(x)$.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Pick $W\in \mathcal{H}$ such that (\ref{eq:thm_B_condition}) holds. Let $V(x)= \exp(W(x))$, which is contained in any maximal Hardy field containing $\mathcal{H}$. Let $U\in \mathcal{H}$ be any function which satisfies $1\prec \log U(x)\prec \log V(x)$. Then by definition of $W$, we have 
    $$
    \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f^{(d)}(x)-p(x)|^{1/d}}{(\log U)'(x)}=\infty
    $$
    for each $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ and each unbounded $f\in \text{Span}\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$, where $d = \deg^*(f)$ (see Definition \ref{def:degree}). So, if each part of Theorem \ref{thm:main} holds for $\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^U$ averages then by Theorem \ref{thm:mikey_thm} each part of Theorem \ref{thm:uniform_main} holds for $\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{unif}}^W$ averages, and vice versa.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def:W_thick}
    Let $W$ be a function with $1\prec W(x)\preceq x$. We say that $S$ is $W$\emph{-thick} if 
    \begin{equation}
        \limsup_{W(N)-W(M)\to\infty}\frac{1}{W(N)-W(M)}\sum_{n=M}^N\Delta W(n)1_{S}(n)=1.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:W_syndetic_equivalence}
     Let $W$ be a Hardy function which satisfies $\log(x)\prec W(x)\preceq x$ and let $S\subseteq \mathbb{N}$. The following are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
        \item $S$ is $W$-thick,
        \item $S^c$ is not $W$-syndetic,
        \item There exists a nondecreasing function $s:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow \mathbb{N}$ with $\lim_{N\to\infty}s(N)\cdot  W'(N)=\infty$ and $s(N)\leqslant N-1$ for all sufficiently large $N\in \mathbb{N}$ such that   $$
        \limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{s(N)}\sum_{n=N-s(N)}^{N}1_S(n)=1.
        $$
        \item There is a sequence of intervals of natural numbers $(I_k)_{k\in \mathbb{N}}$ of the form $I_k  = [a_k,b_k]$ with $\lim_{k\to\infty}(b_k-a_k)\cdot W'(b_k) = \infty$ such that $\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{|S\cap I_k|}{|I_k|}=1$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Each of these statement are identical when $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{W(x)}{x}\in(0,\infty)$, so we assume that $W(x)\prec x$. The equivalence $(i)\iff (ii)$ is clear from the definition, since 
    \begin{align*}
    &\limsup_{W(N)-W(M)\to\infty}\frac{1}{W(N)-W(M)}\sum_{n=M}^N\Delta W(n)1_{S}(n) \\
    = 1-&\liminf_{W(N)-W(M)\to\infty}\frac{1}{W(N)-W(M)}\sum_{n=M}^N\Delta W(n)1_{S^c}(n).
    \end{align*}
    Similarly, statements $(iii)$ and $(iv)$ are restatements of each other.
    
    Next, we show the implication $(iii)\implies (i)$.
     Suppose that $A$ and $B$ are nondecreasing integer valued functions such that $\lim_{N\to\infty}B(N)-A(N)=\infty$ and 
     $$
     \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{W(B(N))-W(A(N))}\sum_{n=A
(N)}^{B(N)}\Delta W(n)1_{S}(n)=1.
$$
Let $s:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow \mathbb{N}$  be a nondecreasing function which satisfies $\lim_{N\to\infty}s(N)\cdot  W'(N)=\infty$ and $s(N)\leqslant N-1$ for all sufficiently large $N\in \mathbb{N}$. We will show that 
$$
\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{s(N)}\sum_{n=N-s(N)}^{N}1_S(n)=1.
$$
In \cite[Equation 4.2]{reilly26}, it is shown that there exists a sequence of nonnegative constants $(c_{N,n})_{N,n\in \mathbb{N}}$ with $\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\in \mathbb{N}}c_{N,n}=1$ such that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:(4)_implies_(5)}
    \sum_{k=A(N)}^{B(N)}c_{N,k}\left(\frac{1}{s(k)}\sum_{n=k-s(k)}^k1_S(n)\right)=\frac{\sum_{n=A(N)}^{B(N)}\Delta W(n)1_{S}(n)}{W(B(N))-W(A(N))}+o_{N\to \infty}(1).
    \end{equation}
Taking $\limsup$ of both sides shows that 
\begin{align*}
    1= \limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{n=A(N)}^{B(N)}\Delta W(n)1_{S}(n)}{W(B(N))-W(A(N))} =& \limsup_{N\to\infty}\sum_{k=A(N)}^{B(N)}c_{N,k}\left(\frac{1}{s(k)}\sum_{n=k-s(k)}^k1_S(n)\right)\\
    \leqslant & \limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{s(N)}\sum_{n=N-s(N)}^N1_S(n),
\end{align*}
and so $\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{s(N)}\sum_{n=N-s(N)}^N1_S(n)=1$. This shows the implication $(i)\implies (iii)$. 
    
    
    Lastly, we show the implication $(iv)\implies (i)$.
     Suppose that there is a sequence of intervals $I_N = [A(N),B(N)]$ such that $\lim_{N\to\infty}|I_N|\cdot W'(B(N))=\infty$ and for each $\varepsilon>0$, $\lim \frac{|S\cap I_N|}{|I_N|}>1-\varepsilon$.

     
Since $\log(x)\prec W(x)$, we know that $\lim_{N\to\infty}B(N)\cdot W'(B(N))=0$ and so (by shortening the intervals $I_N$ if necessary) we assume that $\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{A(N)}{B(N)}=1$ and hence
$\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{W'(A(N))}{W'(B(N))} = 1$.

Next, note that $W(B(N))-W(A(N)) = W'(\xi)\cdot |I_N|$ for some for some $\xi\in I_N$, by the Mean Value Theorem. Then 
\begin{align}\label{eq:MVT}
W'(B(N))\cdot |I_N|\leqslant W(B(N))-W(A(N)) \leqslant &W'(A(N))\cdot |I_N|
\end{align}
and hence 
\begin{align}\label{eq:MVT_2}
W(B(N))-W(A(N)) = W'(A(N))\cdot |I_N|\cdot (1+o_{N\to\infty}(1))
\end{align}
since $\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{W'(A(N))}{W'(B(N))} = 1$. Additionally, 
$$
\lim_{N\to\infty}W(B(N))-W(A(N))=\infty
$$
because $\lim_{N\to\infty}W'(B(N))\cdot |I_N|= \infty$.

For each $\varepsilon>0$ and for all large enough $N$, $I_N$ contains at most $(1-\varepsilon)\cdot |I_N|$ elements of $S$. So 
\begin{align*}
    &\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{W(B(N))-W(A(N))}\sum_{n=A
(N)}^{B(N)}\Delta W(n)1_{S}(n)\\
 \geqslant &\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{W(B(N))-W(A(N))}\sum_{n=A(N)+\lfloor{\varepsilon |I_N|\rfloor}}^{B(N)}\Delta W(n)\\
=& \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{W(B(N))-W(A(N)+{\varepsilon |I_N|})}{W(B(N))-W(A(N))}\\
=&1- \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{W(A(N)+\varepsilon |I_N|)-W(A(N))}{W(B(N))-W(A(N))}\\
\geqslant &1- \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\varepsilon |I_N|\cdot W'(A
(N))}{W(B(N))-W(A(N))} \geqslant 1-\varepsilon
\end{align*}
by (\ref{eq:MVT}). This holds for each $\varepsilon>0$, therefore
\begin{align*}
1=&\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{W(B(N))-W(A(N))}\sum_{n=A
(N)}^{B(N)}\Delta W(n)1_{S}(n)\\\leqslant &\limsup_{W(A)-W(B)\to\infty}\frac{1}{W(B)-W(A)}\sum_{n=A
}^{B}\Delta W(n)1_{S}(n)
\end{align*}
which shows that $(i)\implies(iv)$. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- maintheorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:W_syndetic_main}
    Let $W,f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}$ be functions which satisfy the conditions of Theorem \ref{thm:uniform_main}. Let $(X,\mathscr{B},\mu,T)$ be an invertible measure preserving system and let $A\in \mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A)>0$.
     \begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
        \item Suppose that $\operatorname{Poly}(f_1,\dots, f_{\ell})\cap \mathbb{Z}[x] = \{0\}$. Then for each $\varepsilon>0$, the set 
        \begin{equation}
            \{n\in \mathbb{N}:\mu(A\cap T^{-[ f_1(n) ]}A\cap\cdots \cap T^{-[ f_{\ell}(n) ]}A)>\mu(A)^{k+1}-\varepsilon\}
         \end{equation}
         is $W$-syndetic.
         \item Suppose that $\operatorname{Poly}(f_1,\dots, f_{\ell})$ is jointly intersective.
         Then the set
        \begin{equation}
            \{n\in \mathbb{N}:\mu(A\cap T^{-[ f_1(n) ]}A\cap\cdots \cap T^{-[ f_{\ell}(n) ]}A)>0\}
         \end{equation}
         is $W$-syndetic.
     \end{enumerate}
<!-- end maintheorem-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Suppose that $P$ is not jointly intersective. Pick $r\in \mathbb{N}$ such that there is no $n\in \mathbb{N}$ with $p_i(n)\equiv 0\operatorname{mod} r$ for all $i\in \{1,\dots, \ell\}$. Let $X = \{0,1,\dots, r-1\}$, let $\mathscr{B} = \mathscr{P}(X)$, let $\mu$ be the normalized counting measure on $X$, and let $Tx = x+1\operatorname{mod} r$ for $x\in X$. Taking $A = \{0\}$, it is clear that $R_A(p_1,\dots, p_{\ell})$ is empty.

    Now we suppose that $P$ is jointly intersective and that $(X,\mathscr{B},\mu,T)$ is an invertible measure preserving system. We will show that $R_A(p_1,\dots, p_{\ell})$ is syndetic. Let $W$ be any function which belongs to a Hardy field and satisfies $\log(x)\prec  W(x)\prec x$, and let $f_i = p_i$ for all $i\in \{1,\dots, \ell\}$. Let $f\in \text{Span}\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$ and note that $f\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ and so $\deg^*(f)=0$. Then $W$ is compatible with $f$ and hence property (\ref{eq:W_compatible}) is satisfied. By Theorem \ref{thm:general_thick_szemeredi}, $R_A(p_1,\dots, p_{\ell})$ is $W$-syndetic. This holds for any function $W$ which belongs to a Hardy field and satisfies $\log(x)\prec \log W(x)\prec x$ and so by Lemma \ref{lem:W_syndetic_equivalence} we have that $\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{|R_{A}(p_1,\dots, p_{\ell})\cap I_k|}{|I_k|}>0$ for any sequence of intervals $(I_k)_{k\in \mathbb{N}}$ with $\lim_{k\to\infty}|I_k|=\infty$. It follows that $R_{A}(p_1,\dots, p_{\ell})$ is syndetic, since $|R_{A}(p_1,\dots, p_{\ell})\cap I|>0$ for any long enough interval $I$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
The case $d=1$ immediately reduces to the ergodic Szemer\'edi Theorem. Indeed, in this case the set $\{[ f(n) ]:n\in \mathbb{N}\}$ contains all but finitely many elements of $\mathbb{N}$ and for each $m\in \mathbb{N}$ and each sufficiently large $N\in\mathbb{N}$ there is an $n$ such that $[ f(n) ],[ f(n+1) ],\dots, [ f(n+m) ]$ are each equal to $N$. So, it suffices to consider the case $d>1$. Let $m\in \mathbb{N}$. We will show that 
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:thick_sz_proof}
        R_A(f_1,\dots, f_{\ell})=\{n:\mu(A\cap T^{-[ f(n)]}A\cap T^{-2[ f(n)]}A\cap \cdots\cap T^{-\ell[ f(n)]}A )>0\}
   \end{equation}
   contains an interval of the form $\{n-m,\dots, n\}$. Consider the collection of functions $\mathscr{F} = \{n\mapsto i\cdot f(n-j): 1\leqslant i\leqslant \ell, 0\leqslant j\leqslant m\}$. We will show that $\operatorname{Poly}(\mathscr{F}) = \{0\}$. 
   
   
   Recall the identity $f(n-i) = (1-\Delta)^if(n)$, which makes it clear that \begin{equation}
       \text{Span}(\mathscr{F}) \subseteq  \text{Span}\{\Delta^if:i\geqslant 0\}.
   \end{equation} 
   Recall that $x^{d-1}\prec f\prec x^d$. It follows that for each $i$, $\Delta^if$ tends to either $0$ or $\pm\infty$ and so each nonzero element of $\text{Span}\{\Delta^if:i\geqslant 0\}$ tends to either $0$ or $\pm \infty$. 
   
   Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists a nonzero $p(x)\in \mathbb{R}[x]$ and a $g\in \text{Span}\{\Delta^if:i\geqslant 0\}$ such that $\lim_{x\to\infty}|g(x)-p(x)| = 0$. We know that $\lim_{x\to\infty}g(x) \in \{0,\pm\infty\}$, but $p(x)$ is a nonzero polynomial and so $\lim_{x\to\infty}g(x)=\lim_{x\to\infty}p(x)\neq 0$. It follows that $\lim_{x\to\infty}p(x)=\pm\infty$.
   
   
   Pick $K$ such that $\Delta^Kp(x)$ is constant and apply the Stolz-Ces\`{a}ro  theorem (see Theorem \ref{thm:stolz_cesaro} in Section \ref{section:preliminaries}) $K$ times to see that
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:K_stolz_cesaro}
       1 = \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{g(x)}{p(x)}=\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\Delta g(x)}{\Delta p(x)}=\cdots =\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\Delta^K g(x)}{\Delta^K p(x)}.
   \end{equation}
   We know that $\Delta^K g(x) \in  \text{Span}\{\Delta^if:i\geqslant 0\}$ and so $\lim_{x\to\infty}|\Delta^K g(x)| \in \{0,\infty\}$ which contradicts (\ref{eq:K_stolz_cesaro}) and the fact that $\Delta^Kp(x)$ is constant. Therefore, we have shown that $\operatorname{Poly}(\mathscr{F}) \subseteq \operatorname{Poly}(\{\Delta^if:i\geqslant 0\})= \{0\}$, which is jointly intersective. Pick $W\in \mathcal{H}$ satisfying $W'(N) = (\Delta^{d}f(N))^{1/d}$, so that $\log x\prec \log W(x)$ since $d\geqslant 2$. By Theorem \ref{thm:general_thick_szemeredi}, the set 
   \begin{equation}
       R_1=\left\{n\in \mathbb{N}:\mu\left(A\cap \left(\bigcap_{\substack{1\leqslant i\leqslant \ell\\ 0\leqslant j\leqslant m}}T^{-[ if(n-j) ]}A\right)\right)>0\right\}
   \end{equation}
   is $W$-syndetic. Additionally, from Lemma \ref{lem:W_syndetic_equivalence} and Lemma \ref{lem:derivatives_mod_1} below, it follows that for any $\varepsilon>0$, the set $\{n\in \mathbb{N}: f(n)\operatorname{mod} 1\in (0,\varepsilon)\}$ is $W$-thick with respect to this same $W$. Hence, the set 
   \begin{equation} 
   R_2=\{n\in \mathbb{N}: f(n-j)\operatorname{mod} 1\in (0,\varepsilon)\text{ for all } 0\leqslant j\leqslant m\}
   \end{equation} 
   is also $W$-thick. Then the intersection $R_1\cap R_2$ must be nonempty since the first set is $W$-syndetic and the second set is $W$-thick. Taking $\varepsilon<1/(2\ell)$, for any $n\in R_1\cap R_2$ we have $[ if(n-j) ] = i\cdot [ f(n-j) ]$ for any $1\leqslant i\leqslant \ell$ and $0\leqslant j\leqslant m$, and so 
   \begin{equation}
       \mu(A\cap T^{-[ f(n-j) ]}A\cap T^{-2[ f(n-j) ]}A\cap\cdots \cap T^{-\ell[ f(n-j) ]}A)>0
   \end{equation}
   for each $0\leqslant j\leqslant m$. This shows that the set in (\ref{eq:thick_sz_proof}) contains an interval of the form $\{n-m,\dots, n\}$, which concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:BMR_1}
    Suppose that $f$ is a tempered function which belongs to a Hardy field. Let $p_1(x),\dots, p_k(x)\in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ and put $W = \Delta^{\deg(f)-1}f$. Then for any invertible measure preserving system $(X,\mathscr{B},\mu,T)$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item For any $h_1,\dots, h_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(X)$, the limit 
        \begin{equation}
            \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\text{unif}}^WT^{-[ p_1(\Delta)f(n) ]}h_1\cdots T^{-[ p_k(\Delta)f(n) ]}h_{k}
        \end{equation}
        exists in $L^2(X)$.
        \item For any $A\in \mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A)>0$,
        \begin{equation}
            \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\text{unif}}^W
           \mu(A\cap T^{-[ p_1(\Delta)f(n) ]}A\cdots T^{-[ p_k(\Delta)f(n) ]}A)>0.
        \end{equation}
        \item For any $A\in \mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A)>0$,
        \begin{equation}
           \{n\in \mathbb{N}: \mu(A\cap T^{-[ p_1(\Delta)f(n) ]}A\cdots T^{-[ p_k(\Delta)f(n) ]}A)>0\}
        \end{equation}
        is thick and $W$-syndetic.
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Let $d = \deg(f)$. It is clear that $W=\Delta^{d
    -1}f$ satisfies equation (\ref{eq:thm_B_condition}). Additionally, since $f$ is tempered, we know that $x^{d-1}\log(x)\prec f(x)\prec x^{d}$ and so $\log(x)\prec W(x)$. 
   Let $\mathscr{F} =  \{n\mapsto p_i(\Delta)f(n-j):1\leqslant i\leqslant k, 0\leqslant j\leqslant m\}$ and recall from the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:thick_szemeredi} that $
   \mathscr{F}\subseteq\text{Span}\{\Delta^{i}f:i\geqslant 0\}$ and $\operatorname{Poly}( \mathscr{F})\subseteq \operatorname{Poly}(\{\Delta^{i}f:i\geqslant 0\}) = \{0\}$, which is jointly intersective. Then each conclusion in the theorem statement follows from Theorem \ref{thm:uniform_main} and Theorem \ref{thm:W_syndetic_main}.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:Furstenberg_correspondence}
    For any $E\subseteq \mathbb{N}$ with 
    $$
    \overline{d}(E):=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}1_E(n) > 0
    $$
    there exists an invertible measure preserving system $(X,\mathscr{B},\mu,T)$ and a set $A \in\mathscr{B}$ with $\mu(A) = \overline{d}(E)$ such that for all $n_1,\dots,n_{\ell}\in \mathbb{Z}$
    \[
    \overline{d}(E\cap(E-n_1)\cap\dots \cap (E-n_{\ell})) \geqslant \mu(A\cap T^{-n_1}A\cap \dots \cap T^{-n_{\ell}}A).
    \]
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- maintheorem-4: body_md -->
Let $W,f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}$ be functions which satisfy the conditions of Theorem \ref{thm:main}. Suppose that $\{f_1,\dots, f_{\ell}\}$ satisfies property (\ref{eq:W_compatible}). Let $E\subseteq \mathbb{N}$ be a set with $\overline{d}(E)>0$.
     \begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
        \item Suppose that the conditions of Theorem \ref{thm:main}\ref{condition_1} are satisfied. Then 
        \begin{equation}
            \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\text{unif}}^W(\overline{d}(E\cap (E-[ f_1(n) ])\cap \cdots \cap (E-[ f_{\ell}(n) ])) = \overline{d}(E)^{k+1}.
        \end{equation}
        In particular, for each $\varepsilon>0$, the set 
        \begin{equation}
            \{n:\overline{d}(E\cap (E-[ f_1(n) ])\cap \cdots \cap (E-[ f_{\ell}(n) ])>\overline{d}(E)^{k+1}-\varepsilon\}
        \end{equation}
        is $W$-syndetic. This means that there are ``many" values of $a,n\in \mathbb{N}$ such that $\{a, a+[ f_1(n) ],\dots,a+[ f_{\ell}(n) ]\}\subseteq E$.
         \item Suppose that the conditions of Theorem \ref{thm:main}\ref{condition_2} are satisfied. Then 
        \begin{equation}
            \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\text{unif}}^W(\overline{d}(E\cap (E-[ f_1(n) ])\cap \cdots \cap (E-[ f_{\ell}(n) ])) >0.
        \end{equation}
        In particular,
        \begin{equation}
            \{n:\overline{d}(E\cap (E-[ f_1(n) ])\cap \cdots \cap (E-[ f_{\ell}(n) ])>0\}
        \end{equation}
        is $W$-syndetic. Again, this means that there are ``many" values of $a,n\in \mathbb{N}$ such that $\{a, a+[ f_1(n) ],\dots,a+[ f_{\ell}(n) ]\}\subseteq E$. 
     \end{enumerate}
<!-- end maintheorem-4 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:Boshernitzan_ud}
    Suppose that $f$ is a subpolynomial Hardy function. Then the following are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
        \item $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is u.d. mod 1,
        \item $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f(x)-p(x)|}{\log(x)}=\infty$ for all $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{thm:Boshernitzan_wd}
    Suppose that $f$ is a subpolynomial Hardy function. Then the following are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=$(\arabic*)'$]
        \item $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is w.d. mod 1,
        \item There exists $q(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ and $m\in \mathbb{N}$ such that $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f(x)-q(x)|}{x^{m}}$ is finite and irrational.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
In \cite{Boshernitzan}, the above theorem is stated but only the implication $(2)'\implies (1)'$ is proven. The forward implication is incorrectly cited as being contained in \cite{Boshernitzan_wrong_citation} and it is likely that the correct citation is the preprint \cite{Boshernitzan_preprint}, which was never published. The methods contained in \cite{Boshernitzan_preprint} are largely disjoint from the methods that we consider in this paper, as Boshernitzan uses the existence of Hardy functions which tend to infinity very slowly to show that when condition $(2)'$ does not hold, the sequence $(f(n),f(n+1),\dots, f(n+k))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is dense modulo 1 in $[0,1]^k$ for any $k\in \mathbb{N}$. The authors are unaware of any full proof of Theorem \ref{thm:Boshernitzan_wd} currently contained in published literature.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
Let $(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\subseteq \mathbb{R}$. We say that $(x_n)$ is u.d. mod 1 with respect to $W$-averages if $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^We^{2\pi i kx_n} = 0$ for all nonzero $k\in \mathbb{Z}$.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- maintheorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:W_ud}
     Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field. Let $f\in \mathcal{H}$ be subpolynomial and let $W\in \mathcal{H}$ satisfy $1\prec \log W(x)\prec x$. Then the following are equivalent.
     \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
         \item $\deg^*(f)>0$ and $W$ is compatible with $f$ (Definition \ref{def:W_compatible}).
         \item $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is u.d. mod 1 with respect to $W$-averages.
     \end{enumerate}
<!-- end maintheorem-5 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field and let $f,W\in\mathcal{H}$. Put $d= \deg^*(f)$ and suppose that $d>0$. Then $W$ is compatible with $f$ if $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f^{(d)}(x)-p(x)|^{1/d}}{(\log W)'(x)}=\infty$ for all $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$. 
Taking $W(x)=x$ we find that $W$ is compatible with $f$ if and only if $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f^{(d)}(x)-p(x)|}{x^{-d}}=\infty$ for all $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$. After repeated applications of L'H\^opital's rule, this becomes $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f(x)-p(x)|}{\log(x)}=\infty$ for all $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$. So, Theorem \ref{thm:Boshernitzan_ud} is a special case of Theorem \ref{thm:W_ud}. 
Additionally, \cite[Theorem 1.6]{BKS2025} (see also \cite[Theorem 5.1]{Richter23}) shows that conditions (i) and (ii) of Theorem \ref{thm:W_ud}, along with several other statements, are equivalent but contains the added assumption that $W'$ is nonincreasing.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:gen_wd_condition}
     Let $f$ be a subpolynomial Hardy function. Put $d=\deg^*(f)$ and assume $d>0$.
Suppose that $s:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}$ is a nondecreasing  function satisfying $s(N)\leqslant N-1$ for all sufficiently large $N\in \mathbb{N}$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gen_wd_condition_1}
         \lim_{N\to\infty}s(N)\cdot |f^{(d)}(N)-p(N)|^{1/d}=\infty
\end{equation}
         for each $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$. Then
         \begin{equation}\label{eq:gen_wd_condition_2}
         \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{s(N)}\sum_{n=N-s(N)}^Ne^{2\pi i kf(n)} = 0
         \end{equation}
        for all nonzero $k\in \mathbb{Z}$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Pick $q(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ such that $d=\deg^*(f)=\deg(f-q)$. Then 
\begin{equation} \label{eq:slowest_q}
|f^{(d)}(x)-q^{(d)}(x)|\preceq |f^{(d)}(x)-p(x)|
\end{equation}
for all $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$. Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a maximal Hardy field containing $f$. Pick $W\in \mathcal{H}$ such that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:same_growth_as_f^d}
    \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f^{(d)}(x)-q^{(d)}(x)|}{(\log W)'(x)}=1.
\end{equation}
For example, take 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:example_hardy}
W(x) = \exp\left(\int_0^x |f^{(d)}(t)-q^{(d)}(t)|~dt\right)
\end{equation}
which is contained in $\mathcal{H}$ since maximal Hardy fields are closed under integration and exponentiation. 
For any $U\in \mathcal{H}$ with $1\prec \log U(x)\prec \log W(x)$, $f$ is compatible with $U$ by (\ref{eq:same_growth_as_f^d}) and (\ref{eq:slowest_q}), and moreover $\lim_{N\to\infty}s(N)\cdot (\log U)'(N) = \infty$ by (\ref{eq:gen_wd_condition_1}) and (\ref{eq:slowest_q}). By Theorem \ref{thm:W_ud}, 
\[
\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^Ue^{2\pi i k f(n)}=0
\]
for any nonzero $k\in \mathbb{Z}$ and any $U\in \mathcal{H}$ with $1\prec \log U(x)\prec \log W(x)$, and hence
\[
\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{s(N)}\sum_{n=N-s(N)}^N e^{2\pi i k f(n)}=0
\]
for any nonzero $k\in \mathbb{Z}$. This concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
Theorem \ref{thm:Boshernitzan_wd} follows from Theorem \ref{thm:mikey_thm} and Corollary \ref{cor:gen_wd_condition}. To see why this is true, observe that  $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is w.d. mod 1 if and only if equation (\ref{eq:gen_wd_condition_2}) holds for all $s:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow \mathbb{N}$ with $\lim_{N\to\infty}s(N)=\infty$ and $s(N)\leqslant N-1$ for all sufficiently large $N\in \mathbb{N}$. Now, if $f$ satisfies condition $(2)'$ in Theorem \ref{thm:Boshernitzan_wd} and $\lim_{N\to\infty}s(N)=\infty$ then equation (\ref{eq:gen_wd_condition_1}) automatically holds because $f^{(d)}$ does not tend to $0$. 


For the reverse direction, suppose condition $(2)'$ in Theorem \ref{thm:Boshernitzan_wd} does not hold. Then there is a Hardy function $W$ such that $f$ and $W$ are contained in the same maximal Hardy field, such that 
\[
\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f^{(d)}(x)-p(x)|^{1/d}}{(\log W)'(x)}<\infty
\]
for some $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ (take for example, the function $W$ in (\ref{eq:example_hardy})). Then for this function $W$ we have that $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is not u.d. mod 1 with respect to $W$-averages and hence by Theorem \ref{thm:mikey_thm} there is a function $s$ with $\lim_{N\to\infty}s(N)=\infty$ for which $(\ref{eq:gen_wd_condition_2})$ does not hold.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- example-4: body_md -->
Let $f(x) = x^{3/2}$ and let $s$ satisfy $\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{s(N)}{N^{1/4}}=\infty$, for example $s(N)=N^{1/4+\varepsilon}$ for some $\varepsilon>0$. Then equation (\ref{eq:gen_wd_condition_1}) holds with $d=\deg^*(f)=2$. By the usual proof of the Weyl Criterion, equation (\ref{eq:gen_wd_condition_2}) implies that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:example_2}
         \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{s(N)}\sum_{n=N-s(N)}^N1_{(a,b)}(f(n)\operatorname{mod} 1) = b-a
\end{equation}
         for all $(a,b)\subseteq [0,1]$, where $f(n)\operatorname{mod} 1 = f(n)-\lfloor f(n)\rfloor$ denotes the fractional part of $f(n)$. It follows that for all large enough $N$, there is an $n\in [N-s(N),N]$ such that $f(n)\operatorname{mod} 1\in (a,b)$. This improves \cite[Example 1.10]{reilly26}, and  
         moreover, this is the best possible result of this form since Theorem \ref{thm:W_ud} says that (\ref{eq:example_2}) does not hold if $s(N)$ grows like $N^{1/4}$ or slower.
<!-- end example-4 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:deg=1}
    Suppose that $f$ and $W$ are continuously differentiable and eventually monotone functions such that $1\prec \log W(x)\prec x$, $\lim_{x\to\infty}f'(x)=0$, and the limit $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{f'(x)}{(\log W)' (x)}$ exists in $(0,\infty)$. Then there is a constant $C\in \mathbb{C}$ with $|C|<1$ such that 
    \begin{equation}
        \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^W(e^{2\pi i f(n)}) = C\cdot e^{2\pi i f(N)}+o_{N\to\infty}(1).
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
We begin by considering the special case $f(x) = c\cdot \log W(x)$ for some $c\in (0,\infty)$.
    \begin{align*}
         \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^W(e^{2\pi i 
        f(n)})=&\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^W(e^{2\pi i 
        c\log W(n)}) = \frac{1}{W(N)}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Delta W(n)e^{2\pi i c\log W(n)}\\ = &e^{2\pi i c\log W(N)}\cdot \sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{\Delta W(n)}{W(N)}e^{2\pi i c\log\left(\frac{W(n)}{W(N)}\right)}.
    \end{align*}
The sum $\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{\Delta W(n)}{W(N)}e^{2\pi i c\log\left(\frac{W(n)}{W(N)}\right)}$ is a Riemann sum with partition $\{0<\frac{W(1)}{W(N)}<\frac{W(2)}{W(N)}<\cdots <\frac{W(N)}{W(N)}=1\}$ for the integral $\int_0^1e^{2\pi i c\log(x)}dx$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:riemann_sum}
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{\Delta W(n)}{W(N)}e^{2\pi i c\log\left(\frac{W(n)}{W(N)}\right)}=\int_{0}^1e^{2\pi i c\log(x)}dx = \int_0^1x^{2\pi i c}dx = \frac{1}{1+2\pi i c}.
\end{equation}
It is worth noting that equation (\ref{eq:riemann_sum}) is precisely where we use the assumption that $\log W(x) \prec x$ (or equivalently that $\lim_{x\to\infty}f'(x)=0$), since otherwise $\frac{W(N)-W(N-1)}{W(N)}$ would not tend to $0$ and so the Riemann sum would not tend to the integral. Taking $C = \frac{1}{1+2\pi i c}$ we have 
   \[
   \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^W(e^{2\pi i f(n)}) = C\cdot e^{2\pi i f(N)}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)
   \]
as desired. For the general case, we have that
\begin{equation} \label{eq:f'_asymptotics}
f'(x) = c\cdot (\log W)'(x)+E(x)\cdot (\log W)'(x)
\end{equation} 
for some $c\in (0,\infty)$ and some function $E$ with $\lim_{x\to\infty}E(x)=0$. Let $\varepsilon>0$ and let $N\in \mathbb{N}$ be arbitrarily large. Pick the smallest $N_0\in \mathbb{N}$ such that $\frac{W(N_0)}{W(N)}>\varepsilon/2$ and note that $N_0$ tends to $\infty$ as $N$ tends to $\infty$. Then 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:remove_epsilon_weight}
\left|\frac{1}{W(N)}\sum_{n=1}^{N_0}\Delta W(n)e^{2\pi i f(n)}\right|\leqslant \frac{1}{W(N)}\sum_{n=1}^{N_0}\left|\Delta W(n)\right|<\varepsilon.
\end{equation}
Integrating both sides of equation (\ref{eq:f'_asymptotics}) gives 
\[
\int_n^Nf'(x)dx = f(N)-f(n)
\]
and 
\[
\int_{n}^Nc\cdot (\log W)'(x)+E(x)\cdot (\log W)'(x)~dx = c\log\left(\frac{W(N)}{W(n)}\right) + \int_n^NE(x)(\log W)'(x)~dx. 
\]
Let $u = \log W(x)$ and define the function $\tilde{E}$ by $\tilde{E}(t) = E(W^{-1}(e^t))$. Then $\tilde{E}(t)\to 0$ as $t\to\infty$ and 
\[
\int_n^NE(x)(\log W)'(x)~dx = \int_{\log W(n)}^{\log W(N)}\tilde{E}(u)~du.
\]
For $n\in [N_0,N]$, we have 
\[
\log W(N)-\log W(n) \leqslant \log W(N)-\log W(N_0) =  \log\left(\frac{W(N)}{W(N_0)}\right)
\]
which is bounded uniformly in $N$ by our assumption on $N_0$. Since $\tilde{E}$ tends to $0$, it follows that $\int_{\log W(n)}^{\log W(N)}\tilde{E}(u)~du = o_{N\to\infty}(1)$ uniformly for $n\in [N_0,N]$. Altogether, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:f_difference_asymptotic}
f(n)-f(N) = c\cdot \log\left(\frac{W(n)}{W(N)}\right)+o_{N\to\infty}(1)
\end{equation}
uniformly for $n\in [N_0,N]$. Lastly, using equations (\ref{eq:remove_epsilon_weight}), (\ref{eq:f_difference_asymptotic}), and (\ref{eq:f'_asymptotics}) we have
\begin{align*}
    \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^W(e^{2\pi i f(n)}) &= \frac{1}{W(N)}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Delta W(n)e^{2\pi i f(n)}\\ =& e^{2\pi i f(N)}\cdot \frac{1}{W(N)}\sum_{n=N_0}^{N}\Delta W(n)e^{2\pi i (f(n)-f(N))}+O(\varepsilon)\\
    =&e^{2\pi i f(N)}\cdot \frac{1}{W(N)}\sum_{n=N_0}^{N}\Delta W(n)e^{2\pi i c\log(W(n)/W(N))}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)+O(\varepsilon)\\
    =&e^{2\pi i f(N)}\cdot \frac{1}{W(N)}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Delta W(n)e^{2\pi i c\log(W(n)/W(N))}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)+O(\varepsilon)\\
    =&C\cdot e^{2\pi i f(N)}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)+O(\varepsilon)
\end{align*}
for $C= \frac{1}{1+2\pi i c}$. Taking $\varepsilon\to 0$ completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
\label{thm:d=1_thm}
    Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a maximal Hardy field and let $W,f\in \mathcal{H}$. Suppose that $\deg^*(f)=1$ and that $\log x\prec \log W(x)\prec x$. Then $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is u.d. mod 1 with respect to $W$-averages if and only if $W$ is compatible with $f$, meaning that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d=1_bosh_condition_again}
         \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f'(x)-p(x)|}{(\log W)'(x)}=\infty
\end{equation}
for all $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$.
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
We can replace $f$ by $-f$ if necessary to assume that $f$ tends to $\infty$. For $q(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ the sequence $(q(n)\operatorname{mod} 1)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is periodic and so replacing $f$ by $f-q$ does not affect uniform distribution (see lemma \ref{lem:variant_of_modulo_averages}) and so we assume that $1\prec f(x)\preceq x$. Lastly, we also assume that $f(x)\prec x$ since if 
\[
\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{f(x)-p(x)}{x^n}\in \mathbb{R}\backslash \mathbb{Q}
\]
for some $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$, $n\in \mathbb{N}$ then (\ref{eq:d=1_bosh_condition_again}) holds. We know that $\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{unif}}(e^{2\pi i kf(n)})_{n\in \mathbb{N}}=0$ for all nonzero $k\in \mathbb{Z}$ by Theorem \ref{thm:Boshernitzan_wd} (this is the direction which is proven in \cite[Theorem 1.10]{Boshernitzan}), and so $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^W_{n\leqslant N}e^{2\pi i kf(n)}=0$ for all nonzero $k\in \mathbb{Z}$ by Lemma \ref{lem:wd_means_W_ud} below.

For each $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$, $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{f'(x)}{(\log W)'(x)}\leqslant \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f'(x)-p(x)|}{(\log W)'(x)}$, so it suffices to consider the case $p(x)=0$ in (\ref{eq:d=1_bosh_condition_again}). Since $f$ and $W$ belong to the same Hardy field, the limit $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{f'(x)}{(\log W)'(x)}$ always exists in $[0,\infty)\cup\{\infty\}$. We have three cases to consider
    \begin{enumerate}[label = (\arabic*)]
        \item $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{f'(x)}{(\log W)'(x)}\in(0,\infty)$,
        \item $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{f'(x)}{(\log W)'(x)}=0$,
        \item $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{f'(x)}{(\log W)'(x)}=\infty$.
    \end{enumerate}

    In the first case, (\ref{eq:d=1_bosh_condition_again}) does not hold and we may apply Lemma \ref{lem:deg=1} to see that $\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^We^{2\pi i kf(n)} = Ce^{2\pi i kf(N)}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)$, so $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^W_{n\leqslant N}e^{2\pi i f(n)}$ does not exist. Then the sequence $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is not u.d. mod 1 with respect to $W$-averages in this case.


    (\ref{eq:d=1_bosh_condition_again}) does not hold in the second case either. To see this, pick a Hardy function $V$ such that $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{f'(x)}{(\log V)'(x)}=1$, and note that $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log V(x)}{\log {W}(x)}=0$. One such function is given by 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:maximal_hardy_field_function}
        V(x) = \exp\left(\int_{1}^xf'(t)~dt\right)
    \end{equation}
which is contained in $\mathcal{H}$ since maximal Hardy fields are closed under integration and exponentiation. By Lemma \ref{lem:deg=1}, $
    \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^{V}e^{2\pi i f(n)} = Ce^{2\pi i f(N)}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)$ and in particular, $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{V}_{n\leqslant N}e^{2\pi i f(n)}$ does not exist. By Theorem \ref{thm:mikey_thm} and Remark \ref{remark:1precW} it follows that $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{{W}}_{n\leqslant N}e^{2\pi i f(n)}$ also does not exist. So, $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is not u.d. mod 1 with respect to $W$-averages in this case.


Lastly, in the third case (\ref{eq:d=1_bosh_condition_again}) does hold. Again, consider a Hardy function $V$ such that $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{f'(x)}{(\log V)'(x)}=1$, and note that we now have $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log {W}(x)}{\log V(x)}=0$. Pick any nonzero $k\in \mathbb{Z}$, and observe that $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{kf'(x)}{(\log V)'(x)}\in(0,\infty)$.
By Lemma \ref{lem:deg=1} we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:C_eq}
    \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^{V}e^{2\pi i kf(n)} = Ce^{2\pi i kf(N)}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)
\end{equation}
    for some $C\in \mathbb{C}$ with $|C|<1$. Put $Y_{N,0} = e^{2\pi i kf(N)}$ and $Y_{N,k+1} = \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^{V}Y_{N,k}$ for $N\in \mathbb{N}$ and $k\geqslant 0$. From (\ref{eq:C_eq}) we have that $Y_{N,k} = C^ke^{2\pi i kf(N)}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)$ for all $k\geqslant 0$.  Hence $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^W_{n\leqslant N}e^{2\pi i kf(n)}=0$  by Theorem \ref{thm:mikey_thm} and Remark \ref{remark:1precW}. So $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is u.d. mod 1 with respect to $W$-averages. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:technical_1}
    Let $f: \mathbb{N}\rightarrow \mathbb{R}$ be an increasing function, let $A<B<C$ be elements of $(0,1)$ with $B<C-A$, and let $X<Y$ be natural numbers with $Y-X> \frac{2}{A}$. Suppose that $(\Delta f(n)\text{ mod } 1)\in (A,B)$ for all $n\in \{X,\dots, Y\}$. Then there exist natural numbers $Z,W$  such that $ (f(n)\text{ mod } 1)\in (A,C)$ for all $n\in \{Z,\dots, W\}$ with $X\leqslant Z<W$, $Z< X+\frac{1}{A}$, $W-Z\geqslant \frac{C-A}{B}-2$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
For each $n,k\in \mathbb{N}$, we have $f(n+k)=f(n-1)+\sum_{m=n}^{n+k-1}\Delta f(m)$. Let $k$ be the smallest natural number such that $\sum_{m=X}^{X+k-1}(\Delta f(m)\operatorname{mod} 1)>1$. Then $X+k\leqslant Y$ since $Y-X>\frac{2}{A}$, and the sequence $(f(X)\operatorname{mod} 1,\dots, f(X+k)\operatorname{mod} 1)$ must visit every subinterval of $(0,1)$ which has length larger than $B$. Let $Z\geqslant X$ be the smallest natural number such that $(f(Z)\operatorname{mod} 1)\in (A,C)$, and let $W> Z$ be the smallest number such that $f(W+1)\operatorname{mod} 1\not\in (A,C)$. We know that $W\leqslant Y$ since $Y-X>\frac{2}{A}$. Also, $Z\leqslant X+k<X+\frac{1}{A}$ and 
    \begin{equation}
    (W-Z+2)\cdot B\geqslant \sum_{m=Z-1}^{W+1}(\Delta f(m)\operatorname{mod} 1)\geqslant  C-A,
    \end{equation}
    so $W-Z\geqslant \frac{C-A}{B}-2$. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:shift_inequality}
    Let $g$ be a Hardy function with $\lim_{x\to\infty}g(x)=\infty$ and $\lim_{x\to\infty}g'(x)=0$. Fix any $\varepsilon\in (0,1)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shift_inequality}
        g'\left(x+\frac{1}{g'(x)^{\varepsilon}}\right)=g'(x) \cdot (1+o_{x\to\infty}(1)). 
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
It suffices to show that $\log\left({g'(x+\frac{1}{(g'(x))^{\varepsilon}})}\right)-\log\left({g'(x)}\right)\to 0$ as $x\to\infty$. To this end, consider
\begin{align}
  \log\left(g'(x+\frac{1}{(g'(x))^{\varepsilon}})\right)-\log\left({g'(x)}\right) = \int_{x}^{x+\frac{1}{(g'(x))^{\varepsilon}}}\frac{g''(t)}{g'(t)}dt.
\end{align}
    $\frac{-g''(t)}{g'(t)}$ is a Hardy function which decreases to $0$ and so we have 
    \[
    -\int_{x}^{x+\frac{1}{(g'(x))^{\varepsilon}}}\frac{g''(t)}{g'(t)}dt \leqslant \int_{x}^{x+\frac{1}{(g'(x))^{\varepsilon}}}\frac{-g''(x)}{g'(x)}dt = -\frac{g''(x)}{(g'(x))^{1+\varepsilon}}.
    \]
    For any $c\in (0,1)$ we have that $x^{1+c}g'(x)\to \infty$ as $x\to\infty$ and from this it follows that $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{g'(x)}{x^{-(1+\varepsilon/2)}}=\infty$ and that $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log(g'(x))}{x^c}=0$. So 
    \[
    \lim_{x\to\infty}\left|\frac{g''(x)}{(g'(x))^{1+\varepsilon}}\right| \leqslant \lim_{x\to\infty}\left|\frac{\frac{g''(x)}{g'(x)}}{x^{-(\varepsilon+\varepsilon^2/2)}}\right|=\lim_{x\to\infty}\left|\frac{\log(g'(x))}{x^{1-(\varepsilon+\varepsilon^2/2)}}\right|=0,
    \]
    and hence the desired limit follows.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:derivatives_mod_1}
 Let $f$ be a Hardy function which increases to $\infty$. Let $d\in \mathbb{N}$ with $d>1$ and suppose that $x^{d-1}\prec f(x) \prec x^{d}$. 
 Then for each $\varepsilon>0$, there are arbitrarily large values of $N\in \mathbb{N}$ with $\Delta^{i}f(N)\text{ mod } 1\in (0,\varepsilon\cdot (\Delta^{d}f(N))^{i/d})$ for all $i\in\{0,1,\dots, d\}$. Additionally, $f(n)\text{ mod } 1\in (0,\varepsilon)$ for all $n\in [N,N+K(N)]$, where $K$ is a function that satisfies $\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{K(N)}{(\Delta^{d} f(N))^{-1/d}}=\infty$, and so it follows that the set $\{n\in \mathbb{N}: f(n)\operatorname{mod} 1\in (0,\varepsilon)\}$ is $W$-thick (Definition \ref{def:W_thick}) for $W(N) = (\Delta^{d} f(N))^{-1/d}$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
We begin by noting that $\Delta^df$ decreases to $0$. Let $\varepsilon\in (0,1)$ and let $s:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow \mathbb{N}$ be a function which increases to $\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s_growth}
\lim_{N\to\infty}s(N)\cdot \Delta^df(N)=0\quad \text{ and }\quad \lim_{N\to\infty}s(N)\cdot (\Delta^df(N))^{\frac{d-1}{2d-1}}=\infty
\end{equation}
(for example, one could take $s(n) = (\Delta^d f(n))^{-1+1/(2d-1)}$).
For $n\in \mathbb{N}$, put $A(n) =s(n)\cdot \Delta^df(n)$ and for each $i\in \{1,\dots, d\}$ put 
\begin{equation}
   B_{i}(n) = \varepsilon\cdot \left({\Delta^{d}f(n)}\cdot s(n)\right)^{1-i/d}\quad \text{ and }\quad I_i(n) = \left[A(n),B_i(n)\right].
\end{equation}
Observe that 
\begin{align*}
    \frac{A(n)}{B(n)} = (s(n)\cdot \Delta^df(n))^{1/d}=o_{n\to\infty}(1).
\end{align*}
It follows that the length of $I_i(n)$ is $B_i(n)\cdot (1+o_{n\to\infty}(1)) $. Next, pick an arbitrarily large value of $N_1\in \mathbb{N}$ such that
\[
(\Delta^{d-1}f(N_1-1) \text{ mod } 1) \not\in I_{1}(N_1)\text{ and }(\Delta^{d-1}f(N_1) \text{ mod } 1) \in I_{1}(N_1).
\]
Such a value of $N_1$ exists because $\Delta^{d-1}f$ increases to infinity, $\Delta^{d}f$ decreases to $0$, and the interval $I_{1}(N_1)$ has length much larger than $\Delta^{d}f(N_1)$ when $N_1$ is large enough. From now on, we put $A = A(N_1)$, $B_i = B_i(N_1)$, and $I_i = I_i(N_1)$ for $i\in \{1,\dots d\}$. Note that for each $i\in \{1,\dots, d\}$ we have that $B_i-A>B_{i-1}$ so long as $N_1$ is large enough.  


For $n\geqslant N_1$, the sequence $(\Delta^{d-1}f(n) \text{ mod } 1)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ takes steps of size $\Delta^{d}f(n)\leqslant \Delta^{d}f(N_1)$ and so it follows that $(\Delta^{d-1}f(n) \text{ mod } 1) \in I_{1}$ for $n\in \{N_1,\dots, N_1+K_1\}$, where $K_1$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:K_1_big}
K_1\geqslant \frac{ |I_{1}|}{\Delta^{d}f(N_1)}-2 = \varepsilon\cdot {\Delta^{d}f(N_1)^{-1/d}}\cdot s(N_1)^{1-1/d} \geqslant 2/ A
\end{equation}
when $N_1$ is large enough, since 
\[
\frac{B_1 A}{\Delta^{d}f(N_1)}= (\Delta^{d}f(N_1))^{1-1/d}\cdot s(N_1)^{2-1/d} = ((\Delta^{d}f(N_1))^{(d-1)/(2d-1)}\cdot s(N_1))^{2-1/d}\to \infty
\]
as $N_1\to\infty$.


\textbf{Claim:} There exist natural numbers, $N_1\leqslant \dots \leqslant N_{d}$ and $K_1,\dots, K_{d}$ with $N_{i}\leqslant N_1+\frac{i-1}{A}$ and $K_1\geqslant 2/A$, such that $(\Delta^{d-i}f(n)\text{ mod } 1)\in  I_{i}$ for all $n\in \{N_i,\dots, N_i+K_i\}$ and all $i\in\{1,\dots d\}$.


We prove this claim by induction on $i$. We have already shown the base case $i=1$, and now we show the induction step.

Suppose that $i\in\{1,\dots, d-1\}$ and that $N_i$ and $K_i$ are integers for which we have $(\Delta^{d-i}f(n) \text{ mod } 1) \in I_{i}$ for $n\in \{N_i,\dots, N_i+K_i\}$, where $N_i\leqslant N_{1}+\frac{i-1}{A}$ and $K_i\geqslant 2/A$. By Lemma \ref{lem:technical_1}, there exist $N_{i+1},K_{i+1}\in \mathbb{N}$ such that $(\Delta^{d-(i+1)}f(n) \text{ mod } 1) \in I_{i+1}$ for all $n\in\{N_{i+1},\dots, N_{i+1}+K_{i+1}\}$, where $N_i\leqslant N_{i+1}\leqslant N_{i}+\frac{1}{A}< N_{1}+\frac{i}{A}$ and 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:length_of_interval}
K_{i+1}\geqslant \frac{ B_{i+1}-A}{B_i}-2 =  \left(\Delta^{d}f(N_1)\cdot s(N_1)\right)^{-1/d} \cdot (1+o_{N_1}(1))\geqslant 2/A
\end{equation}
when $N_1$ is large enough. This completes the induction step and the proof of the claim. 


Next, since $N_{d}\leqslant N_1+\frac{d-1}{A}$ we have $N_{d}\leqslant N_1+K_1$ by (\ref{eq:K_1_big}). Thus, taking $N=N_{d}$, we have that $N$ lies in each of the intervals $\{N_i,\dots, N_i+K_i\}$ for $i\in \{1,\dots, d\}$. So,
\[
(\Delta^{d-i}f(N)\text{ mod } 1)\in I_i\subseteq(0,\varepsilon\cdot (\Delta^{d}f(N_1))^{\frac{d-i}{d}}) \text{ for all }i\in\{1,\dots d\}.
\]

Recall that $\Delta^{d}f(N_1)\sim  \Delta^{d}f\left( N_1+\frac{d-2}{A}\right)=\Delta^{d}f\left( N_1+\frac{d-2}{(\Delta^{d}f(N_1))^{\eta}}\right)$ from Lemma \ref{lem:shift_inequality}. Replacing $\varepsilon$ with $\varepsilon/2$ if necessary, we have
\[
(\Delta^{i}f(N)\text{ mod } 1)\in (0,\varepsilon\cdot (\Delta^{d}f(N))^{i/d})
\]
for all $i\in\{1,\dots d\}$. This shows the first claim in the statement of the Theorem. The second claim is immediate by taking $K = K_{d}$ and applying  Lemma \ref{lem:shift_inequality} as above.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
There exist natural numbers, $N_1\leqslant \dots \leqslant N_{d}$ and $K_1,\dots, K_{d}$ with $N_{i}\leqslant N_1+\frac{i-1}{A}$ and $K_1\geqslant 2/A$, such that $(\Delta^{d-i}f(n)\text{ mod } 1)\in  I_{i}$ for all $n\in \{N_i,\dots, N_i+K_i\}$ and all $i\in\{1,\dots d\}$.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
{\cite[Theorem 2.3.7]{Boos}}]\label{lem:silverman_toeplitz}
Let $(\alpha_{N,n})_{N,n\in \mathbb{N}}$ be an infinite matrix with complex entries. Suppose that the following conditions hold.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item For each fixed $n\in \mathbb{N}$, $\lim_{N\to\infty}\alpha_{N,n} = 0$.   
    \item $\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\alpha_{N,n}=1$.
    \item $\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\in \mathbb{N}}|\alpha_{N,n}|<\infty$.
\end{enumerate}
Then $(\alpha_{N,n})_{N,n\in \mathbb{N}}$ defines a {regular matrix method}, meaning that if $(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is a bounded sequence  of complex numbers such that $\lim_{N\to\infty}x_N$ exists then $\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\alpha_{N,n}x_n=\lim_{N\to\infty}x_N$.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:Boos}
    Let $W$ be a function which eventually increases to $\infty$ and let $(\alpha_{N,n})_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ be an infinite matrix of complex numbers which defines a regular matrix method\footnote{The statement of \cite[Theorem 3.2.8]{Boos} assumes that $(\alpha_{N,n})_{N,n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is a \emph{conservative matrix method}, meaning that $\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{N,n}x_n$ exists whenever $\lim_{N\to\infty}x_N$ exists, but it is clear that regular matrix methods are conservative.} (see Lemma \ref{lem:silverman_toeplitz}). Let $c_{N,n} = W(n)\left(\frac{\alpha_{N,n}}{\Delta W(n)}-\frac{\alpha_{N,n+1}}{\Delta W(n+1)}\right)$ for $n,N\in \mathbb{N}$. The following are equivalent.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item $\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{N,n}x_n = L$ for any $L\in \mathbb{C}$ and any bounded sequence of complex numbers $(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ with $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^Wx_n = L$.
        \item  $\limsup_{N\in \mathbb{N}} \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|c_{N,n}|<\infty$, and for each $N\in \mathbb{N}$, $\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\alpha_{N,n}}{\Delta W(n)} = 0$.
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:gauss_stronger_than_weighted}
 Let $W$ be a Hardy function with $\log x\prec \log W(x)\prec x$. Define $\alpha_{N,n} = \frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(n-N)^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}$ for $N,n\in \mathbb{N}$, where $\sigma_N = ((\log W)'(N))^{-1}$ for all $N\in \mathbb{N}$. Let $(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ be a bounded sequence of complex numbers such that $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^Wx_n$ exists. Then  $\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{N,n}x_n = \lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^Wx_n$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
First, we will show that $(\alpha_{N,n})_{N,n\in \mathbb{N}}$ defines a regular matrix method.


By using the substitution $n\mapsto N-n$, we rewrite the sum $ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(n-N)^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}$ as $\sum_{n=-\infty}^{N-1}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{n^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}$. We know that $\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{n^2}{2\sigma_N^2}} = 1+o_{N\to\infty}(1)$ by comparison with the Gaussian integral $\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^2/2}~dx=1$, and we additionally recall that for any $\varepsilon>0$ there exists a constant $A\in (0,\infty)$ such that 
\begin{equation}
    \left|\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{n^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}  - \sum_{n=-\lfloor{A\sigma_N\rfloor}}^{\lfloor  A \sigma_N\rfloor}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{n^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}\right| <\varepsilon. 
\end{equation} 
Using the fact that $\log x\prec \log W(x)$, the inequality $A\sigma_N<N-1$ holds whenever $N$ is large enough and so we also have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:central_limit_theorem}
     \left|\sum_{n=-\infty}^{N-1}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{n^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}  - \sum_{n=-\lfloor{A\sigma_N\rfloor}}^{\lfloor  A \sigma_N\rfloor}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{n^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}\right| <\varepsilon,
\end{equation} 
and hence $\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{N,n}=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{N-1}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{n^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}  = 1+o_{N\to\infty}(1)$. Since $\alpha_{N,n}\geqslant 0$ for all $N,n$, we also have $\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\in \mathbb{N}}|\alpha_{N,n}|<\infty$. Additionally, for each fixed $N\in \mathbb{N}$, $\lim_{n\to\infty}\alpha_{N,n}=0$. Therefore, by Lemma \ref{lem:silverman_toeplitz} we know that $(\alpha_{N,n})_{N,n\in \mathbb{N}}$ defines a regular matrix method. It remains to show that we can apply Lemma \ref{lem:Boos}.

Observe that for fixed $N\in \mathbb{N}$, 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boos_second_condition}
\frac{\alpha_{N,n}}{\Delta W(n)} = O(1)\cdot \frac{1}{\Delta W(n)}e^{-\frac{(n-N)^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}  \to 0 
\end{equation}
as $n\to\infty$ since $W(n)$, and hence $\Delta W(n)$, grows subexponentially.  

Lastly we show that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:messy_sum}
\limsup_{N\in \mathbb{N}} \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}W(n)\left|\frac{\alpha_{N,n}}{\Delta W(n)}-\frac{\alpha_{N,n+1}}{\Delta W(n+1)}\right|<\infty.
\end{equation}
Let $V(n) = \log W(n)$ so that $W(n) = e^{V(n)}$ and $\sigma_N = V'(N)$. Note that $\frac{ W(n)}{ W(n+1)} = e^{-\Delta V(n+1)} = 1+O_{N\to\infty}(\Delta V(n+1))$. Putting $\eta(n)= \frac{W(n)\alpha_{N,n}}{\Delta W(n)}$, we have
\begin{align}
  \notag & \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left|\frac{W(n)\alpha_{N,n}}{\Delta W(n)}-\frac{W(n)\alpha_{N,n+1}}{\Delta W(n+1)}\right| =\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left|\eta(n)-\eta(n+1)(1+O_{n\to\infty}(\Delta V(n+1)))\right|   \\
    \leqslant &\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left|\eta(n)-\eta(n+1)\right| +\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\eta(n+1)\cdot O_{n\to\infty}(\Delta V(n+1)).\label{eq:bounded_sums}
    \end{align}
    The second sum is bounded since
    \begin{align*}
    &\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\eta(n+1)\cdot O_{n\to\infty}(\Delta V(n+1)) \leqslant \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\eta(n)\cdot O_{n\to\infty}(\Delta V(n))  
    \\ =& \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{N,n}\frac{W(n)}{\Delta W(n)}\cdot O_{n\to\infty} \left(\frac{\Delta W(n)}{W(n)}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{N,n}\cdot O_{n\to\infty}(1)= O_{N\to\infty}(1).
    \end{align*}
    To bound the first sum in (\ref{eq:bounded_sums}), observe that the ratio $\frac{\eta(n+1)}{\eta(n)} \sim e^{-\frac{(n+1-N)^2-(n-N)^2}{2\sigma_N^2}} = e^{\frac{-2n-1+2N}{2\sigma_N^2}}$ is decreasing in $n$. This shows that $\eta(n)$ increases to its maximum and then decreases. So $\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left|\eta(n)-\eta(n+1)\right|
 \leqslant  2\cdot \sup_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\eta(n)$. We bound $\sup_{n\leqslant N}\eta(n)$ by noting that 
 \begin{equation*} 
 \frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(n-N)^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}\leqslant \frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}} = \frac{1}{ V'(N) \sqrt{2\pi}} = O_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{W(N)}{ W'(N)}\right) = O_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{W(N)}{ \Delta W(N)}\right).
 \end{equation*}
Then $\sup_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\eta(n) = \sup_{n\leqslant N}\frac{\Delta W(N)}{W(N)}\cdot O_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{W(N)}{\Delta W(N)}\right) <\infty$. We have shown that  (\ref{eq:messy_sum}) holds and so we are done by Lemma \ref{lem:Boos}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- theorem-13: body_md -->
\label{thm:gaussian_dne}
     Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field. Let $W,f\in \mathcal{H}$ and suppose that $\log x\prec \log W(x)\prec x$ and $x^{d-1}\prec f(x)\prec x^d$ for some $d\in \mathbb{N}$, $d>1$.  Additionally, suppose that $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(f^{(d)}(x))^{1/d}}{(\log W)'(x)}=\upsilon\in(0,\infty)$. Then there exist arbitrarily large values of $N\in \mathbb{N}$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gaussian_again}
    \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(n-N)^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}\cdot e^{2\pi i f(n)}= C_{\upsilon}\cdot  e^{2\pi i f(N)}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)
   \end{equation}
   where $\sigma_N = ((\log W)'(N))^{-1}$ and $C_{\upsilon} = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-u^2/2}e^{2\pi i (\upsilon/\ell!)u^{\ell}}du$.
<!-- end theorem-13 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Let $\varepsilon>0$. Put $B = \upsilon/d! = \lim_{N\to\infty}\sigma_N^{1/d}\cdot \Delta^{\ell}f(N)/d!$ and let 
\[
C_{\upsilon} =  \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-u^2/2}e^{2\pi i Bu^{\ell}}~du.
\]
We will find an arbitrarily large value of $N\in \mathbb{N}$ such that 
\[
    \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(n-N)^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}\cdot e^{2\pi i f(n)} = C_{\upsilon}\cdot e^{2\pi i f(N)}+O(\varepsilon)+o_{N\to\infty}(1).
\]
First, rewrite $ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(n-N)^2}{2\sigma_N^2}} e^{2\pi i f(n)}$ as $\sum_{n=-\infty}^{N-1}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{n^2}{2\sigma_N^2}} e^{2\pi i f(N-n)}$. Recall that there exists a constant $A\in (0,\infty)$ such that (\ref{eq:central_limit_theorem}) holds and so it follows from the triangle inequality that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:post_triangle_inequality}
      \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(n-N)^2}{2\sigma_N^2}} e^{2\pi i f(n)} =  \sum_{n=-\lfloor{A\sigma_N\rfloor}}^{\lfloor  A \sigma_N\rfloor}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{n^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}e^{2\pi i f(N-n)}+O(\varepsilon)
\end{equation}
since $(e^{2\pi i f(n)})_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is bounded. Next, recall Newton's backward difference formula, which says that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:newton_formula}
    f(N-n) = f(N)+
    \sum_{i=1}^{m} (-1)^i\binom{n}{i}\Delta^{i}f(N)+O_{N\to\infty}(n^{m+1}\cdot \Delta^{m+1}f(N))
\end{equation}
for any $m\in \mathbb{N}$. By Lemma \ref{lem:derivatives_mod_1}, we pick an arbitrarily large $N\in \mathbb{N}$ such that 
\[
\Delta^{i}f(N)\text{ mod } 1\in (0,\frac{2\varepsilon}{\upsilon A^{d-1}\cdot d}\cdot (\Delta ^{d}f(N))^{i/d})\subseteq (0,\frac{\varepsilon}{A^{d-1}\cdot d}\cdot\sigma_N^{-i})
\]
for all $i\in\{0,1,\dots, d-1\}$. Using the fact that $\binom{n}{i}$ is an integer with $\binom{n}{i}\leqslant  n^i$, we have $\binom{\lfloor A\sigma_N\rfloor}{i}\Delta^{i}f(N)\operatorname{mod} 1 \in (0,\frac{\varepsilon}{d})$ for all $i\in \{0,\dots, d-1\}$ and so 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:small_sum_mod_1}
\sum_{i=1}^{d-1} (-1)^i\binom{n}{i}\Delta^{i}f(N)\operatorname{mod} 1 \in (-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
\end{equation}
for all $n\in \{-\lfloor A\sigma_N \rfloor, \dots, \lfloor A\sigma_N \rfloor\}$. Using (\ref{eq:newton_formula}), replace $f(N-n)$ with 
\[
f(N)+(-1)^{d}\binom{n}{d}\Delta^{d}f(N)+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1} (-1)^i\binom{n}{i}\Delta^{i}f(N)+O_{N\to\infty}(n^{d+1}\cdot \Delta^{d+1}f(N)).
\]
The $O_{N\to\infty}({n^{d+1}\Delta^{d+1}f(N)})$ term is $o_{N\to\infty}(1)$ by L'H\^opital's rule, and (\ref{eq:small_sum_mod_1}) says that  $\sum_{i=1}^{d-1} (-1)^i\binom{n}{i}\Delta^{i}f(N)=O(\varepsilon)$. Additionally, recall that $\binom{n}{d} = n^{d}/(d!)+O(n^{d-1})$ and note that $n^{d-1}\cdot \Delta^{d}f(N) = o_{N\to\infty}(1)$ uniformly for $|n|\leqslant A\sigma_N$ by assumption. Altogether, we have shown that 
\begin{align*}
    e^{2\pi i f(N-n)} =&e^{2\pi i f(N)}\cdot e^{2\pi i (-1)^{d}\binom{n}{d}\Delta^{d}f(N)}\cdot e^{O(\varepsilon)}\cdot e^{o_{N\to\infty}(1)}\\
   =& e^{2\pi i f(N)}\cdot e^{2\pi i (-n)^{d}\Delta^{d}f(N)/(d!)}+O(\varepsilon) +o_{N\to\infty}(1)\\
    =& e^{2\pi i f(N)}\cdot e^{2\pi i (-n)^{d}\cdot B/\sigma_N^{d}\cdot (1+o_{N\to\infty}(1))}+O(\varepsilon) +o_{N\to\infty}(1)\\
    =&e^{2\pi i f(N)}\cdot e^{2\pi i B(-n/\sigma_N)^{d}}+O(\varepsilon) +o_{N\to\infty}(1)
\end{align*}
uniformly for $n\in \{-\lfloor A\sigma_N\rfloor,\dots, \lfloor A\sigma_N\rfloor\}$, and so (\ref{eq:post_triangle_inequality}) becomes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:negligible_terms}
e^{2\pi i f(N)}\cdot\sum_{n=-\lfloor{A\sigma_N\rfloor}}^{\lfloor  A \sigma_N\rfloor}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(n/\sigma_N)^2}{2}}e^{2\pi i B(-n/\sigma_N)^{d}}+O(\varepsilon)+o_{N\to\infty}(1).
\end{equation}

Let $u = -n/\sigma_N$. Then the sum in (\ref{eq:negligible_terms}) is a Riemann sum for the integral 
\[
\int_{-A}^A \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-u^2/2}e^{2\pi i Bu^{\ell}}du= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-u^2/2}e^{2\pi i Bu^{\ell}}du+O(\varepsilon).
\]

This gives us 
\[
    \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(n-N)^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}\cdot e^{2\pi i f(n)} = C_{\upsilon}\cdot e^{2\pi i f(N)}+O(\varepsilon)+o_{N\to\infty}(1).
\]
Taking $\varepsilon\to 0$ shows (\ref{eq:gaussian_again}) as desired.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:negative_result_d>2}
   Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field and let $f,W\in \mathcal{H}$ satisfy $\deg^*(f)=d\geqslant 2$ and  $\log x\prec \log W(x)\prec x$. Suppose that there is $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ such that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gen_bosh_condition_fails}
         \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f^{(d)}(x)-p(x)|^{1/d}}{(\log W)'(x)}<\infty.
 \end{equation}
Then $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is not u.d. mod 1 with respect to $W$-averages.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
By the same reasoning as in Theorem \ref{thm:d=1_thm}, we assume without loss of generality that $x^{d-1}\prec f(x)\prec x^d$. We will show that $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^We^{2\pi i f(n)}$ does not exist. Pick an arbitrarily small $\upsilon>0$ and pick a function $V$ which belongs to a maximal Hardy field containing $\mathcal{H}$ and satisfies $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(f^{(d)}(x))^{1/d}}{(\log V)'(x)}=\upsilon$ (see (\ref{eq:maximal_hardy_field_function})). We will show that $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^Ve^{2\pi i f(n)}$ does not exist and it will follow that $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^We^{2\pi i f(n)}$ does not exist by applying Theorem \ref{thm:mikey_thm} if $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log V(x)}{\log W(x)}=0$. If instead we have $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log V(x)}{\log W(x)}\in(0,\infty)$, then we apply \cite[Lemma 7.1]{uniform_distribution} which says that $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^W_{n\leqslant N}x_n$ converges if and only if $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^V_{n\leqslant N}x_n$ converges under the condition that
$$
\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log V(x)}{\log W(x)} = \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{W(N)\cdot \Delta V(N)}{V(N)\cdot \Delta W(N)}\in(0,\infty).
$$

From Theorem \ref{thm:gaussian_dne} we have \begin{equation}\label{eq:gaussian_again_again}
    \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_N\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(n-N)^2}{2\sigma_N^2}}\cdot e^{2\pi i f(n)}= C_{\upsilon}\cdot  e^{2\pi i f(N)}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)
   \end{equation}
   holds for infinitely many values of $N$, where $\sigma_N = (\log V)'(N)$ and $C_{\upsilon}$ is given by the integral $ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-u^2/2}e^{2\pi i (\upsilon/d!)u^{d}}du$. For this observation to be of any use to us, we need to know that $C_{\upsilon}$ is nonzero. Consider the function $\xi\mapsto C_{\xi} = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-x^2/2}e^{2\pi i (\xi/(d!)) x^{d}}dx$. This function is continuous and it is a classical fact that $C_0 = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-x^2/2}dx = \sqrt{2\pi}>0$. So $|C_{\upsilon}|>0$ so long as $\upsilon>0$ is small enough. Then the limit as $N\to\infty$ of equation (\ref{eq:gaussian_again_again}) does not exist, and hence $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^Ve^{2\pi i f(n)}$ does not exist. This concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- theorem-14: body_md -->
\label{thm:d>1_positive_thm}
    Let $f$ be a Hardy function with $x^{d-1}\prec f(x)\prec x^d$ for some $d\in \mathbb{N}$ and let $s:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow \mathbb{N}$ satisfy 
    $\lim_{N\to\infty}s(N)\cdot (f^{(d)}(N))^{1/d}=\infty$ and $s(N)\leqslant N-1$ for all $N\in\mathbb{N}$. Then (\ref{eq:positive_results_goal}) holds for all nonzero $k\in \mathbb{Z}$.
<!-- end theorem-14 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:positive_result_d>2}
   Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hardy field and let $f,W\in \mathcal{H}$ satisfy $\deg^*(f)=d>1$ and  $1\prec \log W(x)\prec x$. Suppose that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gen_bosh_condition_holds}
         \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|f^{(d)}(x)-p(x)|^{1/d}}{(\log W)'(x)}=\infty
 \end{equation}
for all $p(x)\in \mathbb{Q}[x]$. Then $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is  u.d. mod 1 with respect to $W$-averages.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Pick a nonzero value of $k\in \mathbb{Z}$ and, as in Theorem \ref{thm:d=1_thm} and Corollary \ref{cor:negative_result_d>2}, assume without loss of generality that $x^{d-1}\prec f(x)\prec x^d$.
    
    
    Let $V$ belong to a maximal Hardy field containing $\mathcal{H}$ such that $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(f^{(d)}(x))^{1/d}}{(\log V)'(x)}=1$ (see (\ref{eq:maximal_hardy_field_function})). By Theorem \ref{thm:d>1_positive_thm}, we know that (\ref{eq:positive_results_goal}) holds for each $s:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow \mathbb{N}$ with $\lim_{N\to\infty}s(N)\cdot (\log V)'(N) = \infty$ and $s(N)\leqslant N-1$ for all sufficiently large $N\in \mathbb{N}$. By (\ref{eq:gen_bosh_condition_holds}) and our choice of $V$, $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log W(x)}{\log V(x)}=0$ and so by the implication $(3)\implies(2)$ in Theorem \ref{thm:mikey_thm}, we have $\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{n\leqslant N}^We^{2\pi i k f(n)}=0$. This holds for all nonzero $k\in \mathbb{Z}$, and so we have shown that $(f(n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is u.d. mod 1 with respect to $W$-averages.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- theorem-15: body_md -->
\label{thm:vdc_trick}
Let $(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence of real numbers and let $(I_N)_{N\in \mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence of intervals of natural numbers with $|I_N|\to\infty$ as $N\to\infty$. Suppose that for each $j\in \mathbb{N}$, 
$$
\frac{1}{|I_N|}\sum_{n\in I_N}e^{2\pi i x_{n+j}}\overline{e^{2\pi ix_n} }=\frac{1}{|I_N|}\sum_{n\in I_N}e^{2\pi i (x_{n+j}-x_n)}\to 0
$$
as $N\to\infty$. Then $\frac{1}{|I_N|}\sum_{n\in I_N} e^{2\pi ix_n} \to 0$ as $N\to\infty$.
<!-- end theorem-15 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:second_derivative_vdc_inequality}
    Let $F$ be a real-valued function which is twice continuously differentiable on an interval $I$ and suppose that for some $\lambda>0$ and $\alpha\geqslant 1$, we have $\lambda \leqslant |F''(x)|\leqslant \alpha \lambda $ for $x\in I$. Then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:second_derivative_vdc_inequality}
    \left|\sum_{x\in I}e^{iF(x)}\right|\ll \alpha \lambda^{1/2}|I|+\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}}.
    \end{equation}
    Here the notation $A\ll B$ means that there is an absolute constant $C$ such that $A\leqslant CB$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:better_vdc}
    Let $k$ be a positive integer and $K=2^k$. Assume that $I=(X_1,X_1+X]\subseteq (X_1,2X_1]$ and let $S= \sum_{x\in I} e^{if(x)}$. For any positive $H_1,...,H_k\leqslant C(k)\cdot X$, where $C(k)$ is a constant depending only on $k$, we have 
    \begin{align}\label{eq:vdc_inequality}
\left(\frac{S}{X}\right)^K \leqslant 8^{K-1} \left(\sum_{i=1}^k\frac{1}{H_i^{K/2^j}}+\frac{1}{XH_1\cdots H_k}\sum_{h_1=1}^{H_1}\cdots \sum_{h_k=1}^{H_k}\left|\sum_{x\in I(\mathbf{h})}e^{if_1(x)}\right|\right)
    \end{align}
    where $f_1(x) =f(\mathbf{h},x)=h_1\cdots h_k \int_0^1\cdots \int_0^1 \frac{\partial^k}{\partial x^k }f(x+\mathbf{h}\cdot\mathbf{t})d\mathbf{t}$, $\mathbf{h}=(h_1,\dots,h_k)$, $\mathbf{t}=(t_1,\dots ,t_k)$ and $I(\mathbf{h})=(X_1,X_1+X-h_1-\dots-h_k]$.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- theorem-16: body_md -->
\label{thm:better_vdc}
    Let $j\geqslant 3$ and let $f$ be a real valued functions which is $j$-times continuously differentiable on the interval $I = (X_1,X_1+X]\subseteq (X_1,2X_1]$, for $X,X_1\in \mathbb{N}$. Suppose that $f^{(j)}$ is monotone on $I$ and that there are constants $\lambda>0$ and $\alpha\geqslant 1$ with $\lambda \leqslant |f^{(j)}(x)|\leqslant \alpha\lambda $ for all $x\in I$. Then 
    \begin{align}\label{eq:better_vdc}
        \left|\frac{1}{|I|}\sum_{x\in I}e^{if(x)}\right|\ll \left(\frac{1}{X}\right)^{\frac{1}{2^{j-2}}}+\alpha^{\frac{1}{2^{j-2}}} (\lambda X^{j-2})^{\frac{1}{2^{j-1}}}+\left(\frac{1}{\lambda X^{j}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2^{j-1}}}
    \end{align}
    and the absolute constant depends only on $j$.
<!-- end theorem-16 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
We will begin by applying Lemma \ref{lem:better_vdc}. Let $k=j-2$, $K=2^k$, and put $H_i = \min\{\frac{X}{C(k)},\frac{X}{2k}\}$ for $i\in \{1,\dots, k\}$ and $H_{\times} = [1,H_1]\times \dots \times [1,H_k]\subseteq \mathbb{N}^k$. It is clear that 
    \[
    \sum_{i=1}^k\frac{1}{H_i^{K/2^i}}\ll \frac{1}{X}
    \]
    and so it suffices to bound the second term in (\ref{eq:vdc_inequality}). Observe that $\lambda h_1\cdots h_k \leqslant |f_1{''}(x)|\leqslant \alpha\lambda h_1\cdots h_k$ for all $x\in I$ and $\mathbf{h}=(h_1,\dots, h_k)\in H_{\times}$. Therefore, we may apply Lemma \ref{lem:second_derivative_vdc_inequality} to see that for each $\mathbf{h} \in H_{\times}$, 
    \begin{align*}
        \left|\sum_{x\in I(\mathbf{h})}e^{if_1(x)}\right| \ll& \alpha \lambda^{1/2}(h_1\cdots h_k)^{1/2}(X-h_1-\cdots -h_k)+\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}(h_1\cdots h_k)^{1/2}}\\
        \leqslant & \alpha \lambda^{1/2}(h_1\cdots h_k)^{1/2}X+\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}(h_1\cdots h_k)^{1/2}}.
    \end{align*}

    So far, we have shown that 
    \begin{align}
